BURIBOY A K H M E D O V
TIMUR THE GREAT
(His Life, Social and Political Activities)
ABDULLA KADYRI NATIONAL HERITAGE PUBLISHERS 
TASHKENT-1996

This booklet, written on the basis of historical compositions and 
documents of the 13th—15th centuries, tells 
of the epoch 
when 
Amir Timur, the outstanding statesman and great general, patron of 
science and culture, lived; it acquaints the reader with his life full 
of emotional shocks, anxiety and danger 
and with his social and 
political work.
It is intended for historians and general public interested 
in 
the ancient history of our independent Motherland.
Translated by Olga OVCHINNIKOVA

INTRODUCTION
Thank Goodness, that the “ Red E m p ire ”, called the 
U SSR, collapsed, and having taken our fate into our own 
hands, we have acquired the possibility to study 
our 
ancient and rich history and lofty culture of the old t i ­
mes, to restore our spiritual values, using the lessons of 
the past for transform ing our society. The most im p or­
tant point is that we have gradually started 
to 
learn 
thinking independently and openly express our opinion, 
In connection with this, our President Islam Karimov’s 
speech 
at 
the 
first 
session 
of 
the 
01 iy 
Majlis 
on 23 February 1995 is of particular significance for us: 
“N ow ”, the President said, “our main goal is to teach 
our 
people 
to think 
independently, 
to assert 
their 
self-confidence. For if thinking and 
consciousness are 
not free from enforcement and pressure, M an cannot be 
free” . Yes, it is really so. From these words of our h o ­
noured President each of us m ust draw the right conclu­
sions for himself or herself. And the sooner we do this, 
the better.
Timur was also a representative of 
humankind, like you 
or me, and it follows that 
whoever he m ight have been, ho­
wever wise and enterprising a politician he might have been,, 
he had his share of mistakes and shortcomings. While doing 
a lot of good, he also committed errors. 
It m ust be a 
true saying: “ To err is h u m a n ”. Besides, one is always 
to bear in m ind that Timur w as a representative of the 
rulin g class. He was enthroned by this ruling class. So 
it is but natural that he served this 
feudal class 
and 
stood for its interests, in fact, there has 
never 
been a 
single king or supreme ruler that did not serve his own 
class. Amir Timur 
w as no exception to the rule.
It is true that Timur w ag ed wars, like all 
the monarchs before and after him. The reasons are that, to be­
4 -2 5 2

gin with, the feudal class w a s interested in w a g in g w a rs 
s n d annexing territories of other peoples. Feudal leaders 
needed loot — one of the sources of wealth- And without 
wars, one could not get the booty, and hence — wealth. 
The other point is that all the rulers of the past 
had a 
common feature — aspiration for conquests to dem ons­
trate their strength an d power. In the course 
of 
such 
wars, seas of blood were w antonly shed, great sacrifices 
were m ade and m any fell victim, towns 
and 
villages 
were destroyed. It w as also Amir T im u r’s way.
However, a thorough analysis of historic events shows 
th at as a result of military marches to Iran, Azerbaijan. 
Iraq and Syria, u n dertaken by Timur in 1381— 1404, he 
put an end to feudal discord in Iran, A zerbaidjan 
and 
Iraq and to internecine w a rs of long standing. W ith the 
defeat of T ukhtam ysh-khan in 1391 — 1395, the 
military 
and political might of the Golden Horde, th a t h a d kept 
Asia an d Europe in 
the vices of constant menace, w a s 
ruined. It is this that accelerated the liberation of Russia 
from the M on g o ls’ yoke by more th an 300 years. F in a l­
ly, as a result of defeat 
of a m ighty 
Turkish 
Sultan, 
Bayazid the Lighting-Wielder, in 1402 the peoples of the 
Balkan peninsula found their freedom from the O tto m an 
Turkish yoke, and so were West E uropean countries set 
free from the menace of their attack.
The most important result of these w ars for the his­
tory of mankind is the restoration of economic and cul­
tural ties between the countries of the F a r E a st and the 
M editerranean, which had existed since ancient 
times 
but were interrupted after G enghiz-khan’s invasion.
We consider it Amir T im u r’s great service to world 
history.
Timur put an end to the feudal disunity that prevailed in 
M a v e ra n n a k h r and Turkestan, he set 
the land free from the 
Mongol yoke and founded a powerful centralized state. H e 
resurrected the ruined towns and cities and created con­
ditions for further development 
of agriculture, 
crafts 
and trade. In this w ay there appeared soil for develop­
m ent of science and culture. The epoch 
of Timur 
and 
the Timurids is recognized as the “ Renaissance 
of the 
E a s t ”, which is a witness to a great role 
of Timur in 
the history of the peoples of M a v e ra n n a k h r and Turkes­
tan.

SOCIAL AND POLITICAL SITUATION IN CHAGATAI 
ULUS IN THE 30— 60ies OF THE 14th CENTURY
Amir Tim ur’s youth fell upon the time 
of the sh a r­
pening of the feudal internecine strife 
in the C hagatai 
Ulus*, which resulted in a grave economic and political 
crisis. Instead of jointly ruling the country, the beks of 
the ulus were in constant enmity. At the time when the 
menace of Mongol invasion loomed over the land, m any 
of them scattered in various directions 
and 
the 
rest 
went to serve the Mongol Khan** Timurbek, who had just 
come to the historic arena with a hundred jighits***, found 
himself alone a gainst the powerful enemy. So w h at w as 
he to do? Certainly, with his h undred warriors, he could 
not stand against a strong enemy like Tugluk 
Timurkhan. But it w a s a disgrace to scurry after people like 
Khaja barlas. Thus, he decided to join 
the k h a n ’s ser­
vice temporarily, to win his trust, gain some post, and 
then, seizing power, try to protect his native land from 
occupation and plunder. And he did win the K h a n ’s con­
fidence, but not in the w ay Bayazid jalayir did — not 
by crawling on his knees, but with his honour and dig­
nity kept intact. Tugluk Tim ur-khan appointed him the 
ruler of the Kesh region. By using his power efficiently, 
Timurbek m a n a g e d to keep a considerable 
part of the 
country from Mongol enslavement, and the people from 
plunder and humiliation.
To u n d e rsta n d how tragic those years were 
for the 
peoples of M a v e ra n n a k h r and Turkestan, an d w hat suf­
ferings Amir Timur 
w as experiencing 
by 
constantly 
being in the saddle, one m ust cast one’s m in d ’s eye over 
history, in particular, the history of the C h agatai Ulus.
B eginning with the year 1227, the C h a g a ta i U lus w a s 
a feudal state encom passing a large territory. It included
* Ulus is a feudal domain.
** Khan is a supreme ruler.
*** Jighits are young warriors.

U ig u ristan (East T urkestan), the lands of the Seven Ri­
vers (Semirechye) an d the Ili river oases, M averannakhr, 
the regions of the S y rd ary a and south of the A m udarya.
But the Ulus w as not developing as an independent 
state: on the contrary, it happened to get into 
a 
very 
grave situation. In mid 13th century, after 
1251, to be 
exact, owing to the 
stre n g th e n in g 
of the 
internecine 
stru g g le in the Mongol Empire, the C h a g a ta i U lus w a s 
divided between the kahan* M u n k a-kah an (1251 — 1260) 
an d the khan of the Golden Horde Baty (1227— 1255). 
A t that time E a st Turkestan, the Seven Rivers basin and 
the Ili river valley, as well as N orth-East F e rg a n a , were 
placed under M unka-kahan, while M av eran n ak h r, T u rk e s­
tan, W est F e rg a n a and Khorezm got under the influence 
of the Golden Horde. Officials of the Golden H orde — 
tanmachi** and darukhachi*** were appointed 
to 
the 
cities and towns of M av eran n ak h r.
But the C h a g a ta id s did not submit to the officials of 
the kahan and the Golden Horde. Algu, of the C h a g a ta id 
stock, seized the throne, took power (1261 — 1266) 
and 
began a determined stru g g le for 
liberating 
the 
ulus 
from the oppression of the Golden Horde. According to 
the data quoted by the 
historians Rashididdin and V a s ­
sal, Algu-khan, h a v in g started an open fight a g a in s t the 
domination of the Golden Horde, even m a n a g e d to drive 
Mongol troops out of the towns of M av eran n ak h r. A nd 
A lgu-khan did not stop at that. H a v in g 
mustered an 
army, he beg an a m arch to get the towns along the S y r ­
darya banks, and 
even routed the Mongol troops at the 
tow ns of Otror.
It is true, too, th at even after these victories, the C h a ­
gatai Ulus 
did not gain independence, although 
there 
were periods of its com parative rise. For example, it ob­
tained a certain degree of independence during the rule 
of Barak-khan (1266— 1271). But it did not last long. U n ­
der C hag a ta i-k h a n ’s 
g ra nd son Yusun-Kara, 
the kaan 
an d the Golden H orde restored their domination 
over 
the C h ag atai Ulus.
In the rule of D uva-khan (1291 — 1306) and Kepakkhan 
(1318— 1326), however, the C h ag atai Ulus re-es­
tablished its former state again. 
Duva-khan 
provided
* 
Kahan is a khan-in-chief of the Turkic and Mongolian tribes
** Tanmachi are military chiefs.
*** Darukhachi are mayors, or magistrates.

amenities for towns and cities, for instance, he restored 
A ndijan and m ade it the capital of F e rg a n a 
province. 
He m ade a compact with a strong khan, Khaidu by n a ­
me, and undertook several m arches onto the lands that 
were under the rule of the kahan, 
he also interfered in 
the interior affairs of the White Horde. In his turn, Kepak-khan introduced reforms of the country’s adm inis­
tration, consolidated the state machinery and improved 
the economic and financial state of the country.
After the death of Kepak-khan, the C hagatai 
Ulus 
w as again caught in the vices of feudal w a rs and s tr u g ­
gle for power. It is sufficient to say, that within the year 
1326 alone, two sons of Duva-khan Ilchigdai and DuvaTimur ascended the throne one after the other. And with­
in 1334— 1342, the throne w as nominally 
occupied by 
Buzan, Chankshi, Isan-Timur, whereas actually the po­
wer wras fully concentrated in 
the h a n d s 
of the tribal 
noblemen.
So, the result of all this w as that, by the forties of the 
3 4th century, the C h ag atai Ulus split, forming two se­
parate state — M ogolistan and M averannakhr. 
M ogo­
listan 
comprised 
East 
Turkestan 
(K ash g ar), 
The 
Seven Rivers, the Ili river oasis-lands, and East Fergana. 
M av eran n ak h r included the western' part 
of the Ulus, 
with some regions south of the Ainudarya, and SouthEast Khorezm, together with the cities of Khiva and Kyat.
Kazan-khan 
(1338— 1346) 
m ade 
attempts 
to s u p­
press feudal internecine discord in the western part of 
the Ulus, mostly in M averannakhr, but did not succeed 
in it. On the contrary, he himself fell victim to an in­
trigue started by the chiefs of the nomadic tribes, led by 
an influential Emir K azagan. After the death of Kazankhan, the fate of the Ulus w as in the han d s of Emir K a ­
zagan. But he 
could not become an absolute sovereign 
of the whole country either, for he was not of the 
Genkhizid 
blood. 
Therefore, 
D anishm andcha-khan 
(1346— 
3348) was 
put 
on the throne, 
and 
Bayankuli-khan 
(1348— 1357) succeeded him. But they, too, 
were 
only 
regarded and treated as khans, while in fact all the po­
wer in the Ulus w as in the h a nd s of the emirs. 
After­
w a rd s they enthroned Timur-Shah (1357— 1359), but the 
latter could not become the absolute ruler either. 
!
In' 1359, as a result of the internecine struggle o f ‘the 
feudal chiefs, the C hagatai Ulus split into s-mall frag ­

ments. Khaji barlas, chief of the lands of Kesh and its 
districts, 
declared himself 
an independent 
ruler. 
In 
Khojent — Bayazid jalayir, in Tatkent 
and Saripul —• 
Khizr Yasavuri, in Kukhistan — E m ir 
Satylmish, 
in 
K h u r a s a n — E m ir Kaikhusrav, in A rk h a n g sarai — Uljaitu Aperdi, in Balkh — Uljai-Buka 
suldus,

Aperdi, 
also

o th e r’s lands and rob the people. As a result, the country

cumstances.
And it w a s right at that time (1360) that, like a new 
illness onto a sick body, Tugluk Timur, 
the 
khan 
of 
Mogolistan, attacked M av e ra n n a k h r — he invaded the 
country at the head of a big army. The 
“ independent” 
emirs ra n to w hatever safe places they could 
find, in ­
stead of stan d ing up united to defend their motherland. 
A nd Bayazid jalayir and a few other emirs went bow ing 
to the invaders, humbly seeking to serve them.
It is in those days, so try in g 
for the country, 
th at 
Timurbek, a twenty-four-year-old brave youth in the peak 
of his prime, appears on the arena of history. H e did not 
run aw ay with those who left his own people to the e n e ­
m y ’s plunder in the days so hard for his M o th e rla n d — 
the people who had given him food and water; he shared 
all his people’s hardships. This is how 
he 
remembers 
those days of his life: “ The descendant of Genghiz-khan 
T ugluk Timur-khan, intending to seize 
the country 
of 
M a v e ra n n a k h r and h av in g crossed 
the river 
Khojent 
with his troops, sent a m e ssa g e to me and emir Khaji 
barlas, co m m an d in g th at we should come before h i m . .. 
Khaji barlas asked for my advice. He proposed 
either 
to go with his people to K horasan, or to go 
to T ugluk 
Timur-khan. I advised him this way: “ If you go to Tugluk-khan, there will be two benefits and one harm. E s ­
caping to K horasan will involve two harm s and one b e ­
nefit” . H e did not heed my advice and left for Khorasan. 
As to me, at first I hesitated where to go — whether 
to 
K horasan or to Tugluk-Timur-khan. As I w as in indeci­
sion, I sent a letter to my spiritual teacher 
with a re ­
quest to give m e advice as to how to act. 
And he 
sent

m e the following answer: 
“A m an asked the fourth khalif, his m ajesty Ali ibn Abu Talib, m ay G o d ’s grace a t­
tend him: if H eaven be the arc of the bow, E arth — the 
thread of the bow, events — the arrow, people — their 
target, and the archer — the Most H igh himself — may 
His might increase! — where can people 
run 
to save 
themselves?” To this the khalif replied: “ Let people run 
under the protection of the Most High. Acting in a simi­
lar way, you, too, now run to Tugluk Timur-khan 
and 
take the bow and arrow aw ay from him ” .
H avin g re-read his spiritual tutor’s letter several ti­
m es and weighed all, Timurbek found his advice 
to be 
right, and, to protect his subjects’ interests, and peace 
a n d quiet of his motherland, he set his steps to the M o n ­
gol khan. A nd in this w ay he m a n a g e d to retain at le­
ast some part of power and become the shield 
for the 
people of M averannakhr, protecting it from plunder and 
humiliation. In the end, h aving come 
to the 
Mongols, 
gradually, as time passed, he did m a n a g e 
to take 
the 
bow and arrow out of their hands. Soon he became the 
closest advisor of the khan. The khan started acting on 
his advice. 
This is w hat “The C O D E ” * says about 
it: 
“ When Tugluk Timur-khan crossed the w aters of Khojent he sent the emirs Bekchik, Khajibek, U lu g Tuktem ur an d other leaders of Mongols at the head of three 
m ilitary detachments to rob the country. The c onsequen­
ces were woeful for the land. M any cities, towns and vil­
lages of M av e ra n n a k h r were burned 
and plundered by 
th e Mongols. They had to be stopped one way or another. 
Choosing the right moment, Timurbek entered the K h a n ’s 
a p a rtm e n t and addressed him, requesting th a t the deeds 
of the greedy robbers should be stopped. The Khan i m ­
mediately appointed an 
official who w a s 
to return ail 
th e loot, all th a t had been taken away, back to the o w ­
ners; and he forbade the emirs to invade 
the interior 
of M a v e ra n n a k h r in future” .
In 1360 the forces of Tugluk Timur-khan really did 
not invade far into M averannakhr, but already 
a - year 
later, in 1361, they occupied the entire country. The beks 
of the Ulus did not demonstrate loyalty to their Mother*
* 
“The CODE” — a famous set of laws by Timur “The Code o f. 
Timur” is meant.

land, exactly as before. Only Timurbek, using his power, 
became the shield of his M otherland 
again, 
protecting 
his people from robberies and violence.
FIGHT AGAINST MONGOLS
In 1362 Tugluk Timur-khan left M a v e ra n n a k h r in a 
hurry, passing the power in the land to his son Iliyaskhoja-oglan, as there w a s unrest 
in Mogolistan 
and a 
m utiny of a group of emirs. H ardly had the khan m a n a ­
ged to cross the 
river Syrdarya, as 
Ilyaskhoja-oglan, 
together with Em ir Bekchik an d other favourite emirs, 
conspired to remove Timurbek from the affairs of state, 
and if a convenient occasion presented itself, 
to bodily 
annihilate him. The intrigues were m ore an d more en­
hanced and turned dangerous. F in d in g himself in these 
circumstances, Timurbek w as forced to leave the khan's 
palace and escape to the mountains. ..
In those years, when disunity and instability reigned 
in the Ulus, two active and enterprising emirs found their 
w a y to each other; they were Timurbek and the late Ka* 
z a g a n ’s grandson Emir Khusayn. In search of luck, try­
ing their fortune, they went tow ards Khorezm. But they 
did not find any luck. Moreover, in a battle at Khiva they 
were defeated by the ruler 
of those lands 
TavakkalaKongurot, and, with the rem ainder of their w arrio rs and 
servants, they had to retreat far into the desert. 
For a 
long time they were w a d in g through the severe, torrid 
sands, and finally, when they had already just come out 
to the M akhm udi Aul in the area under the authority of 
Makhan, they were taken prisoner by the people of Ali­
bek Janikurban. For 62 days were they kept in dungeon 
by Alibek. According to Historian Sharafiddin Ali Yazdi, 
Alibek intended to sell Timurbek and K husayn to Irani­
an merchants. But in those days, not a single carav an 
passed Makhan. The prisoners were miraculously saved 
by Alibek’s elder brother Emir Mukhammad-bek.
In 1361 — 1364 Timurbek and Emir K husayn lived on 
the south bank of the A m u d ary a in the regions of Kakhm ard, Daraghez, Arcif and Balkh, 
w a g in g 
guerrilla 
w a r against Mongols. They would catch the mom ent and 
cross to the right bank of the 
river, they 
would even 
assault the Mongol forces stationed in Termez, 
at the 
Jron Gates, in H u z a r and Kesh. In 1363— 1364 they even

won a num ber of victories over Mongols. And in the au ­
tumn of 1362, Timurbek and Emir K husayn were ligh­
ting in the w ar a gainst Malik K utbiddin’s bitterest and 
cruellest enemies, supporting this Seistan ruler. Tim ur­
bek, accustomed to be in the front lines of the fighters, 
was wounded in the right arm and leg in one 
ol 
the 
battles, and remained lame to the end of his days ever 
since.
After Mongols were driven out 
of M a v e ra n n a k h r in 
]364, Timurbek and Khusayn came to an agreem ent and 
put Kabul-shah of the C h a g a ta id stock on the throne of 
the Ulus. -
The Mongols, however, did 
not accept 
the 
defeat. 
Jliyaskhoja-oglan would not lose a country 
like M ave­
rannakhr, and, m ustering a strong force, 
he began an 
offensive against M a v e ra n n a k h r and Turkestan ’ in the 
spring of 1365. At that time Timurbek and Emir K hu­
sayn did not have sufficient forces for the w ar against 
the Mongols. Despite this, as soon as they got to know 
that the Mongols m ade a camp near Sairam, Timurbek and 
Em ir Timur 
stood up 
a g ain st Iliyaskhoja-oglan. 
At 
dawn of 22 M ay 1365, there w as a bloody battle at Chinaz. But by midday, when Timurbek broke into the right 
flank of the Mongol arm y and victory w as close, Emir 
Khusayn procrastinated and by this let Timurbek down: 
instead of attacking the left flank of the enemy forces, 
Khusayn did not move from his positions. More than this, 
he then left the battle-field and retreated to the left bank 
of the river. In addition to all this, the sky became a m ass 
of dark cloulds and a downpour began, which in a trice 
turned the battle-field into a swamp, where horses got 
stuck in the mud. The downpour was so heavy that the 
warriors could not even lift up their heads. Mongol w a r ­
riors were saving themselves under cover 
of 
woollen 
felt carpets, called koshma, and thick woven 
strips of 
carpet, called palas, whereas Timurbek and K husavn's 
jighits found it very hard to bear. U nder these 
condi­
tions it was impossible to continue fighting 
with 
the 
enemy forces superior in numbers. So, trying to keep his 
men from senseless peril, Timurbek w as also forced to 
leave the battle-field, retreating to the left bank of the 
Syrdarya. In history this battle goes under the nam e of 
“Janghi Lai”, or “Mud Battle” .
in the meanwhile, Iliyaskhoja-oglan who did not meet

any resistance, occupied Khojent, Jizak and a num ber 
of other cities of M averan n ak h r, and continued his march 
to Sam ark an d . As he knew the situation in the city, he 
hoped to take it without bloodshed. At that time S a m a r ­
kand really seemed powerless to stand up a g ainst a big 
force: it did not have either high walls, or solid fortifica­
tions, or an o u ts ta n d in g emir, or well-armed warriors. 
But supposing 
it w as so, Iliyaskhoja-oglan 
w as 
g r a ­
vely mistaken. He did not take into account the determ i­
nation of the people. It w a s the people who took up the 
defence and shouldered it, and it w a s M av la n a z a d a, 
a 
m a d r a s a h tutor, A bubakr Kalavi, a craftsman, an d Khurdaki Bukhari — a dead-shot who led the people. They u n i­
ted all the layers of citizens around themselves, o r g a n i­
zed the defence of the city, armed the people, set b a r r a g e 
in the widest streets and a rra n g e d ambushes 
in m a n y 
spots. To make a long story short, the defenders of S a ­
m a r k a n d offered stubborn resistance to Mongol invaders

ta x on the people of a different faith (jizzya) w as ab o ­
lished.

AGGRAVATION OF THE U LU SES EMIRS’ 
INTERNECINE DISCORD
Emir Khusayn, like his g randfather Kazagan, strove 
t o obtain sovereign power, intending to attain the throne 
of the C hagatai Ulus, while Timurbek stood a gain st vio­
lation of the custom that had been established 
am ong

K husayn did not 
belong to the “white

that Khusayn w as not to become the Khan. At the sam e

m ade by

He who is plotting to deceive 
Is sure himself to turn to dust,
He who by cunning sets a snare 
Is doomed to fall into his trap.
W hen my letter reached Em ir Khusayn, he w a s extre­
mely em barrased and begged to forgive him, but I did 
not believe him a second tim e”.

FORMATION OF TIMUR’S STATE
In the month of Tirmah, 771 year of the Hijra (July 
1370), Amir Timur convened a great kurultai in S a m a r ­
kand. According to tradition, 
oglans, 
novons ** and 
emirs from all over the Ulus were invited to participate. 
Except for Zinda Chashm, ruler 
of Shibirgan 
region, 
all the emirs of the Ulus arrived. It w a s a witness to the 
fact that all the tribes and people living in the area b e t­
ween the great rivers of S yrdarva and 
A m udarya ex­
pressed their voluntary recognition of the power of Suyurgatm ysh-khan 
(1370— 1388) 
and Amir Timur.
* 
Kurultai is a convention of 
emirs, dignitaries 
and "tribal 
■chiefs.
** Noyon (rank) is a prince, chief of a big military 
detach­
ment, like a lumen.

Ai the Kurultai in S a m a rk a n d they discussed both 
the tasks of forming central governm ent an d those of 
creating an army. W ithout doing this, it would have been 
impossible to put an end to feudal disunity, 
to ensure 
peace and tranquillity in the country, and, 
as a result, 
to achieve development of agriculture, crafts, tra d e and 
culture, that is to consolidate the economic and political 
state of the country.
In the m atters of the state government, Amir Timur 
abided by twelve rules: 1) to act according to the canons 
of Islam and Shariat, m aintain and promote the religion 
of Islam; 2) in ruling the country, to lean upon all the 
population layers: sayids, sheiks, scholars an d learned 
people, that is, upon educated people, upon officials, arti­
sans, traders and other subjects, and protect their inte­
rests; 3) to govern the state by counsel and meetings, to 
be enterprising, active, prudent and economical; 
4) to 
rule the state an d society on the basis pf the 
existing 
law s and customs; 5) to encourage those diligently do­
ing their state duty, to rew ard emirs and officials with 
posts, ranks and other state decorations as their merit 
deserves; 6) to conduct the affairs of state fairly and a s 
conscience dictates, to defend the needy from the t y r a n ts ’’ 
oppression; 7) to treat sheiks, ulems* and 
far-sighted, 
wise people with respect and honour; 8) in one’s work, 
to act firmly and decisively, after h av in g considered all; 
not to undertake a new task before the old one is done; 
9) alw ays to be in the course of events, 
to know 
the 
state of one’s subjects and common people, and protect 
and defend them from the oppression on the part of r u ­
lers and officials; 10) to defend and respect those who 
arrived from other countries in search of shelter; 11) to 
rem ember the good deeds and services once done, 
to 
remember children, kith and kin, and to pay in coin for 
kindness; 12) to take care of state officials, respect and 
value them.
In the course of his thirty-five-year-long social and 
political labour, Sakhibkiran Timur strictly abided by the­
se twelve rules, and, in his turn, found strong support of 
people of all walks of life.
Before starting a new venture, Amir Tim ur 
would 
consult his mentors, ulems *, dignitaries and noblemen.
* Ulem is a Moslem scholar

“Although the outcome of a venture m ay be veiled 
by 
the curtain of fate”, 
he would say, “one should not n e g ­
lect the opinion and sensible advice of a wise and p r u ­
dent 
man. I accomplished each deed consulting people 
who had grown wise 
with experience.” However, 
not 
every piece of advice m ay be useful. Amir Timur knew 
this "very well. He could tell a real adviser from a false 
one. He seldom m ade mistakes in his work. 
Almost no 
one could take him in. He could unerringly tell the truth 
from the lie, and see truthfulness and falsehood. Amir 
Timur belonged to the type of 
ruler who realises 
the 
immense role of advice in governing a state. In particu­
lar, one should specially m ark the following 
w ords of 
his: “ Nine tenths of all the affairs of the state I would de­
cide with the help of counsel and conference, and only 
one tenth — by m ean s of the sword.” 
These 
w ords of 
Amir Timur ring true and urg en t today.
Amir Timur especially stressed th at the state should 
lean upon four factors: power, treasury, officials, and 
people. It is really so. The stability of a state depends on 
these four factors in a great degree.
Power. The fate of the country and the 
life 
of the 
subjects greatly depend upon the central state machinery 
and its activity, that is, on 
how and who actually go­
verns the state. Amir Timur clearly realised this. To b e ­
gin with, he shaped his state machinery — it w as not 
num erous but efficient. His attention w as concentrated 
on the quality, not on the quantity: he appointed honest, 
hard-working and enterprising people.
It should be first of all noted that T im u r’s state w as 
governed by only 
seven vesirs *: The ministries 
were 
headed by honest emirs tested in various circumstances: 
Davud, S arbuga, K husayn barlas, Akbuga, khaji Makhmudshah, Elchibuga b a khadur and Davlatshah-bakhshiThe work of reorganizing the army w a s entrusted 
to 
Em ir Jak u barlas, 
Khaji 
Saifiddin, 
Abbas-bakhadur, 
Emir Iskandar, Ahlam-shaih, 
Alaf-kavchin, 
Ardashirkavchin, Kary inak, Khitai-bakhadur, Snaih Ali-bakhadur, 
Tabon-bakha'dur, Bakhti-shah and others.
To the post of vesir, Amir Tim ur 
appointed 
noble, 
wealthy, smart, honest and enterprising people. In his 
opinion, a vesir w as to possess three 
qualities: 
noble

birth; discernment; constant aw areness and knowledge 
of the subjects’ and a r m y ’s condition; patience a n d p e a ­

te, he warned.

and

for

In court, the sons, g randsons and other relatives of

Emirs played a great role in the arm y command. Out

Treasury. The treasury was mainly formed 
by the 
taxes paid by the population, contributions from the con­
quered countries and cities, returns from the state lands 
and other property. “ Diminution of the tre a s u ry ” , 
the 
Code says,” will ultimately bring about the dispersion 
of the army, which will finally weaken State Pow er.”
According to the data from historical sources, in m e ­
diaeval states, including that of Timur and the Timurids, 
the population w as liable to the following taxes: kharaj 
(land tax); 
main jikhat (tax in cash 
and in k i n d ) ; ikhajat 
(tax for the court’s expenses); ta g a r (duty from the po­

and crafts), baj (customs duty); sar-i shum ar 
(polltax,

and savurin 
(a special collection from the population to present

Troops. The army plays a topical role in the fate of 
the crown and state. Conscientious and loyal service of 
the warriors to their M otherland depends greatly on the 
situation of the force, on the w a rrio rs’ 
life and on the 
way they are provided for. Therefore, sovereigns always 
paid particular attention to the circumstances the army 
was in. Amir Timur gave a special attention to the mili­
tary and showed constant 
care for them. 
In the years 
before him, warriors mainly lived on the income they got 
from their loot and the rare gifts of the rulers. But Ti­
mur appointed pay to each warrior in conformity with 
his post and duty. The rank-and-file soldier got the price 
of his horse, if he did his duty properly; a monthly cash 
upkeep of a bakhadur equalled the price of two to four 
horses; C omm ander in charge of a group of ten warriors 
received ten times more per month than each of his w a r ­
riors; comm ander of a hundred — twice more than the 
commander of ten; comm ander of a thousand —■ three 
times as much as that of a h u n d r e d . . . The commander 
of a tumen — ten thousand w arriors — got three times 
more than the head of a thousand. . . And the allowance 
of the Great Emir was ten times more than that of all 
the warriors under him. Besides, warriors, particularly 
com manders of ten, hundred, thousand w arriors and tumanboshi (head of a ten-thousand force) also got ulufe 
(cash for provisions and for their horse’s fodder).
It should be noted that before departure for the war, 
the warriors were paid 
their salary 
in advance — for 
six-seven months ahead 
(and before the cam paign against 
Bayazid the warriors were given their salary for seven 
years ahead at once); immediately before battle they got 
special gifts called ukulka. W arriors also got their share 
of profit from the booty the force had gained.
If a warrior got old and could not take part in action 
any longer, Amir Timur showed care for him and 
his 
descendants. He kept explaining to his children, vesirs 
and commanders: “ Let elderly warriors, wise from their 
experience, be respected and honoured, and asked for a d ­
vice. .. May they be treated as the pillars of the state; 
and after them, their sons should be given the posts of 
their fathers.”
It should also be marked that it was actually Amir 
Timur who laid down the foundations of a well armed

and well trained army. H e instructed his emirs in the 
fundam entals of strateg y and tactics. Besides the g uard 
of the court, special g u a rd forces and num erous nukers*, 
a special ten th ousand strong force of the guards, his 
arm y mostly included 
the w arriors mobilized in the ulus 
and all the parts of the state before the beginning of a 
campaign. The assembly of the forces was 
mostly 
laid 
upon the emirs of the uluses, regions and districts.
The army of Tim ur an d the Timurids resembled the 
armies of U ghiz-khan and Genghiz-khan in its o r g a n iz a ­
tion. It consisted of cavalry and infantry forces and was 
subdivided into tens, hundreds, thousands and ten thou­
sands of warriors. The army w as assembled only before 
the beginning of the 
campaign. 
There was a 
special 
“ artillery” force in T im u r’s army, which included manjaniks, rahdandoz, naftandoz — the catapult, stone-hurlers 
and inflammable mixture devices and throwers.
Subjects (citizens). The stability of State Power de­
pends, in no small degree, on the subjects, on the citi­
z e n s’ conditions an d their loyalty 
to that power. 
The 
existence of the state, as well as its disappearance, extinc­
tion, is determined by its subjects, that is, by common 
people. It is not accidental, then, that Amir Timur tre a ­
ted both his officers and all his subjects fairly, follow­
ing the dictates of his conscience, and sought to satisfy 
common people. It is clearly seen from his taxation poli­
cies. The land liable to taxing by kharaj (landtax) was 
usually divided into three categories: the size of kharaj 
from the first category land m ade one-fourth of the harvest, 
th at of second category — two harvars*; that 
of 
the 
third category — one harvar. If a citizen would not agree 
to pay the tax in this m anner, then one h arv ar of wheat 
w a s evaluated 
at 5 silver zolotniks 
(about 
4.25 gr), 
one harvar of barley — 2.5 zolotniks, and the tax from 
him w as taken in cash. In separate cases 
Amir Timur 
bid to exempt his subjects from tax or duty, for example, 
from giving accommodation to a m bassadors and m essen ­
gers who were p a ssing through the villages; from peshkash and savurin — duty from the population gathered

are 
warriors, 
servants, 
earlier 
— 
guards.

to present donations to padishahs and high-placed peo­
ple when they passed the localities; and from shilon-puli
— to a rran g e festivities for those in authority. In general 
Amir Timur called upon tax-collectors, village and com ­
munity elders lo ireaf common people 
in fairness and 
conscience, and avoid illegal actions against them.
There was one more population estate, whose separate 
representatives oppressed the working people, 
sucking 
their blood like leeches. They were buyer's-up. Actually, 
merchants are an estate that society needs. They bring 
from far-away countries what their own lacks, 
making 
the life of the people easier, that is, in 
case they 
sell 
their goods honestly. But there are such traders as spe­
culators, and there arc, unfortunately, too m any of these 
and, as the great writer Navoi wrote, “there is anything 
in their shops except conscience and honour” . Amir Ti­
mur was merciless towards speculators. 
Here is what 
Claviho said on this point: “That day (October 9, 1404), 
Timurbek gave an order to make answ erable some b u t­
chers for selling meat at too high prices during his ab ­
sence. Other traders who had sold their goods at high 
prices were also punished.”
FIRMNESS OF LAW
The stability and might of any state, peace and well­
being of its country and people are linked with the s t a ­
bility arid firmness of the laws that are in force in it.
Like other peoples, the Uzbek people is known to have 
had, 
from times immemorial, the laws that 
regulated 
family, property, inheritance relations between the state 
and its citizens. On the basis of these laws the citizens 
and society were defended by legal 
institutions 
(the 
judge, state control (supervision), police, etc.), the offi­
cials called mukhtasib (an official checking 
the obser­
vance of the shariat regulations by the Moslems), esaul, 
policeman and other officials. According 
to historical 
sources, earlier at the time of Timur and 
the Timurids, 
there were three classes of judges: the judge, abiding by 
the Shariat in his work; the akhlos (the 
judge who ac­
ted on the basis of the customs and traditions of the time 
in considering the cases), and kazi askar — 
a military 
judge. In their activities the judges were guided not only

by the Shariat regulations and the customs and tr a d i­
tions that existed at that time, but 
also had 
in their 
h a n d s the texts of the canons and laws written by the 
m ajor Moslem lawyers.
At the time of Timur and the Timurids, 
they 
were 
also guided by a special code, connected with the nam e 
of great Timur himself — “ The Code of T im u r” , where 
were clearly and distinctly laid down he rules of b e h a ­
vior for all citizens, and the duties of the officials and the 
rulers, the rules of governing the state and the troops. To 
be exact, “The Code of T im u r” is a valuable code of laws 
for governing the state and the army, the country and 
the society.
Amir f i m u r w as right to note that “ if the state is not 
based on religion and common law, is not consolidated 
by the law and codes, this kingdom loses its m agnificen­
ce and brilliance. A nd such a kingdom 
is like a naked 
man, at the sight of whom everybody turns his eye away, 
and this kingdom is like a house without roof, doors or 
bar, wherein anyone may enter” .
Indeed, “ The C ode” defines the rights and 
duties 
of all, from Padishah to common people.
Amir Timur clearly pointed out the duties and obliga­
tions of every officer of state. For instance, vesirs under 
the divanbeghi were responsible for the general condi­
tions in which the citizens and the troops lived, as well 
as for the financial state of the country and state insti­
tutions’ activities. “Wise is the vesir” , “ The Code” reads, 
“who performs the work of governm ent starting from the 
circumstances, patiently and with tact, and by m eans of 
his sensible measures, installs law 
and order 
in 
the 
state”.
The rights and duties of emirs are distinctly defined 
in “ The Code” . The emirs 
are one of the pillars of the 
state. Like the vesirs, they must be of noble birth, 
and 
they m ust possess such qualities as discernment, courage, 
spirit of enterprise, cautiousness 
and economy; 
they 
m ust conduct the affairs 011 h av in g considered all the 
consequences of each step they take. They m ust “know 
the secrets of conducting a battle, the ways of scattering 
the enemy forces, and not lose heart at the peak of the 
combat; they m ust lead the troops without shade of trem» 
bling or hesitation, and if the fighting order is broken, 
they must be able to restore it without delay” .

In appointing a m an to a post, Amir Timur 
exacted 
loyalty and 
fidelity from each. 315 men who had been 
side by side with him from the very 
beginning 
of his 
career and taken part in his battles, were appointed to 
high 
posts by 
him. A hundred 
of them 
were 
m ade 
com manders of tens, a hundred — comm anders of h u n ­
dreds, a h u n d re d — comm anders of thousands. Out of the 
remaining fifteen, four were appointed to the posts 
of 
high beks, one — to that of Great Vesir, the rest — to 
other high posts of the State.
As “ The Code” says, the law is one and the same and 
equal for all, for the vesir and the emir, for the khokim * 
and a common subject. There are a lot of data 011 this 
point in different sources. For example, 
the 
ruler 
of 
West Iran and Azerbaijan (from 1393) Em irzade * Miran sh ah wholly abandoned himself to am usem ents and 
orgies and neglected his state affairs — and for 
this 
Amir Timur dismissed him from his post, and sentenced 
his companions to capital punishm ent and executed them. 
Sharifiddin Ali Yazdi mentions the fact that during the 
seven-year w ar the ruler of the Farce district, 
Pirmukham m ad Sultan, Tim ur’s grandson, who showed ta r d i­
ness in action, was punished in the same way. Also, as 
“Zafar-nam e” has it, when Amir Timur 
was returning 
from the seven-year war, the people of Shibirgan block­
ed his way and complained of the local ruler who was 
oppressing his people; then, on the orders of Timur, this 
khakim was hanged by his feet. The following informa­
tion from “The Code” also confirms this thought: 
“ If 
the vesirs of finances, who are treasurers of the country, 
committed a financial 
abuse and appropriated a share 
of the funds for themselves, it m ust be investigated: if 
the sum appropriated is equal to the sum 
of the 
uluf 
(salary) due to him, then let this sum be given him as a 
gift. However, if the sum appropriated 
is 
twice 
the 
am ount of his salary, the extras m ust be deducted from 
his salary. If the appropriated sum 
exceeds 
his 
s a ­
lary three times, it must all be taken from him for the 
benefit of the treasury” .
Along with this, Timur stipulated the protection of the 
warriors and common people by the law, 
pointing out

** Emirzade is a prince, a king's son.

that 
.. if village and community elders do harm to the 
common people and in this way cause 
them 
damage, 
these elders must he sentenced to fine, so mm s a's urate with 
the d am age caused, and must pay the dam ages to ihosp 
harm ed (the plaintiffs), for the latter to he able to return 
to the former state. If the village elders or the co m m u ­
nity cause violence to the people of lower estate, these 
elders must he fined, with the fine being 
equal to the 
violence caused. If a tax-collector or a khakim causes 
or allows violence and ruins the people, these individuals 
must bear the respective punishment". It is well known 
that law 
breaking 
and 
violence 
over 
people

with 
the 
law, 
too, 
and 
the law 
ran: 
“ . . If

warrior 
should exceed his rights and cause violence to a com m o­
ner, that warrior should be detained and passed into the 
h ands of the commoner he had wronged, for the latter to

Timur spread the protection by

only u n t o 
his own people, but also upon the peoples of the c o u n ­

to 
protect them from imprisonment and being plundered.
Amir Timur w as trying to keep the society clean and 
uphold high moral principles in the people. "If a pferson 
breaks or knocks out somebody's teeth, blinds one, cuts 
an ear or nose, drinks wine, commits adultery, that p e r­
son is to be brought before the judge, 
who shall 
take 
decisions according 
to Shariat or according to common 
law.” Of importance, too, were the laws, rules and re g u ­
lations on beg g ars and beggary. It is worth noting that 
in these the emphasis is laid not on strength, but mi per­
suasion, “The C ode” reads: “ • . . B eg g ars must be g a t h ­
ered in one place and given out food; 
also some kind 
of occupation should be assigned to them. 
They 
must 
all be branded, for them not 
to go begging 
in future.

be exiled or sold into

country”.
Timur pointed out that a criminal 
should 
only be 
punished on the basis of the law, after his guilt being e s ­
tablished and confirmed in the presence of four w itnes­
ses. Even if grave crimes a gainst the State 
had been

committed hv high born people, vesirs of high 
rank .or 
emirs, one must not punish them 
in haste, 
but 
their

be thoroughly examined, advised Timur. 
Inviolability of the law, rules and regulations very much 
depends on the people’s u n d erstan din g them, on his a t­
titude to them. To achieve this, ii w as necessary to spread 
the juridical knowledge am ong 
the people, 
especially 
laying stress on the principles of the Shariat 
and the 
fundamentals of Islam. The state of Timur and the Timurids paid a lot of attention to this. “ For the purpose 
of teaching the faithful Moslems the principles of reli­
gion and the rules and regulations of Shariat and Islam, 
instructing in tafsir*. khadis** and fikh***, 1 
a p p o in ­
ted ulema**** and 
mudarrisses***** “ to each 
town", 
said Amir Timur.
From the data given above, it is clear that Amir Ti­
mur created a centralised, powerful feudal state, gover­
ned by law. This slate was known and recognized in all 
the world in his time.
FIGHT 
FOR THE UNITY OF CHAGATAI ULUS

and

WARS WITH THE JUCHI ULUS
Up to 1377, the Juchi Ulus was 
also 
divided 
into 
two independent states — the White Horde and the Gol­
den Horde. The Golden Horde (with the capital of Sarai 
Berka) formed the right wing 
of the ulus, 
including 
North-East Khorezm, Northern Caucasus, 
Bulgar (the 
Volga-Kama princedom), West Siberia and the Crimea. 
The White Horde (with the capital of Signak) m ade the 
left w ing of the Juchi Ulus, comprising the lands of the 
lower reaches of the Syrdarya river from Yangikent to 
Sabran, and the steppes on the banks of S yrdarya from 
Ulu tau 
(Great Mountain) to Senghir yagach, as well 
as the lands from Karatal to Siberia. In the seventies of 
the fourteenth century, Urus-khan the ruler of the White

Horde (1361 — 1375) attempted to unite the two parts of 
the Juchi Ulus and revive its former honour and glory. 
But the fighting between the Ju gid s themselves and the 
feudal lords of Dasht-i 
kipchak did 
not make it pos­
sible for him to attain this aim. It m ust be noted, 
that 
Amir Timur did not wish the reunion of these two parts 
either. If the Juchi 
Ulus had become united, as it used 
to be at the time of Batu-khan. Berdi-khan and Uzbekkhan, it would have been difficult to preserve the inde­
pendence of M averannakhr. Therefore, from his first days 
as a ruler, Timur began to fight against the unification 
of the two uluses. He did all possible 
to put his 
own 
m an on the 
White Horde throne. First of all, he began 
to interfere in the enhanced feudal struggles in the W h i­
te Horde, and in every way he supported 
Tokhtamyshoglan, whose father had been killed by Urus-khan. 
He 
took Tokhtamysh under his protection several times and 
showed his care for the m an and his attention to him. f i ­
nally, with the help of Amir Timur, Tokhtamysh took the 
throne of the White Horde.
Amir Timur w as overjoyed by this event, as he hoped 
that Tokhtamysh would conduct his affairs, seeking his 
counsel, and would conduct his, T im u r’s policy. 
But it 
did not at all happen as Tim ur 
had 
expected. 
Soon 
Tokhtamysh-ogian forgot his benefactor and his hospi­
tality and confidence. And when Tokhtamysh seized the 
Golden Horde, he developed a hostile policy 
tow ards 
Tim u r’s state.
Amir Timur m ade three marches a gainst Tokhtamysh 
in 1389, 1391 and 
in 1395. Yet he never tried 
to gain 
supremacy over the Juchi Ulus. In w a g in g 
these wars, 
he pursued only one goal — to break down the military 
and political power of the Golden Horde 
and, 
in this 
way, to gain security for the countries which were under 
the authority of his own state- If Timur had sought to e s ta b ­
lish his dominion over the Juchi Ulus, then in 1395, af­
ter the complete routing of Tokhtamysh, when such cities 
as 
Sarai-Berka, 
K hadj-tarkhan 
(A strakhan), Saraichik 
were lying at his feet, he would not have given this rich 
booty to Koirichak-oglan, the son of Urus-khan.
Let us make a short survey of the reasons and the 
results of these three ca m p a ig n s of Timur 
a gainst the 
Golden Horde.
At the end of 790 of the Hijra (December 1, 1388 by

the Gregorian calendar), Tokhtamysh-khan sent his n u ­
merous forces under Ilpigmysh-oglan to M averannakhr. 
Without meeting any resistance, the Dashtikipchaks got 
over the Syrdarya in the place of Achchik near Djizak 
(Jizak) and m ade camp there. In spite of a severe frost, 
Timur assembled all the forces he had 
and moved 
to­
w ard s Jizak. As soon as he approached Khojent, U m arshaih, who had come from Andijan, joined 
his 
forces 
with those of his father. To make the long story short, 
T im u r’s army, surrounding the troops of Ilpigmysh-og 
lan in Achchik, defeated them. H aving spent the remain 
ning part of the winter in the district of Ikara, early in 
March 1389, Timur continued his cam paign against Tokh­
tamysh-khan. But Tokhtamysh, having avoided an 
openfield battle, retreated far into the interior of Dasht-i kipchak. The Timurid forces giving blow after blow, pursued Tokhtamysh-khan to A vrang chakal and Al-tamga. 
But as at this very time M ogolistan troops 
began 
to 
accumulate near the borders 
of 
M averannakhr, 
the 
m arch was interrupted.
The campaign a gainst the Juchi Ulus was resumed 
in two years, in 1391. Timur was well prepared for it: 
in the autum n of 1390, Tinmrbek transferred his head* 
quarters to Chinaz and announced the total mobilization. 
In the spring of 1391, he began a big cam paign against 
Tokhtamysh. it continued for six months and ended with 
the defeat of Tokhtamysh troops on Ju n e 18, 1391, at the 
place of Kunduzchi (K undurcha), located between S a m a ­

Tokhtamysh saved his life by escaping with the men 
who remained alive. But some time later he m a n a g e d to 
re-establish the former might of the Golden Horde. The 
important point is that he never gave up his intenion to

tured Shirvan and other cities of Azerbaijan- The same

his

“From this moment on, Tokhfcamysh cannot be tru s te d ”, 
Timur concluded, and in F e b ru a ry 1395 he began a war

against him. The forces of both sides came 
to a clash 
on the banks of the Terek 
river. This w ar also ended in 
T im ur’s victory. Tokhtamysh ran to Bulgar. Timur had 
won this victory th anks to the new tactics he used in the 
battle.
To pay in coin for Tokhtam ysh’s plundering and destroying many cities 
and settlements 
of M a v eran n ak h r 
during his absence, and even b u rn in g Zanjir-sarai, one

At that time Amir Tim ur was trying to find Tokhta­
mysh all over Russia; he went to the city of Fyazan, seized 
the city of Yelets, and then turned towards Moscow, but 
did not attempt to capture 
it, and from 
the banks of 
the Oka he turned back. This w ar caused considerable 
d a m ag e to the lands of Azak, Kuban and CherkessiaWARS WITH OTHER COUNTRIES, THEIR REASONS 
AND CO NSEQUENCES
In the period from 1381 to 1404, Amir Timur 
u n d e r­
took cam p aig n s in Iran, Azerbaijan, Iraq, Syria and In ­
dia several times. He also went to Georgia 
and other 
lands of the Caucasus. If his activities are viewed from 
the point of social and economic development and ideo^ 
logy of his own time, one can see that there were objective reasons for it.
Firstly, all padishahs, khans and emperors were from 
the same ruling class. And this means, then, that they 
toed the line of that class, were obliged to serve its in­
terests and aims. Otherwise, they would not be kept in 
power and would be dethroned. This ruling class appro­
ved of only those padishakhs, khans and emperors who 
constantly led w ars of conquest, occupied newrer and n e ­
wer countries, for it was the only way they could expand 
their possessions and gain rich spoils.
Secondly, in those times it was considered a matter 
of prestige with rulers to expand o n e ’s estate by seizing 
other people’s lands, it w as a sign of knighthood, If one

takes a mental view of history, it will be seen that not 
only Timur, but also m an y other great sovereigns like 
Darius, Alexander the Great, 
Julius Cesar, 
Genghizkhan, followed the same way. At a closer look, Napoleon 
Bonaparte, Peter I an d the subsequent tsars of Russia 
did the same, too. It w as not said in vain that 
“ Kings 
are the shadows of God on the earth.” However, 
such 
conquerors 
as Darius 
I, Alexander 
the Great, 
Genghiz-khan and Amir Timur deemed that “ if there is one 
God in the world, then the shadow that embodies him on 
the inhabited part of the earth must also be one king.” 
“ I told myself”, Amir Timur said, “that God is one, and 
he has no companion. But if that is so, then the 
ruler 
m u st also be one. One padishah!” — an d at that 
very 
m om ent Baba Ali-shah came in and said: “ Hey, Timur! 
The Almighty pointed out that if there are two gods on 
the earth and in heaven, the world will come to ruin.” 
A nd I heeded his words. I started to seek my fortunes by 
reading 
the Holy Koran and came across these verses: 
“ Inno Ja a ln a k a khalifatan fi-l-arz” , which means: “We 
appoint thee khalif on the e a rth ”.
Thirdly, Amir Timur strictly abided by the teaching 
of the Holy Koran and the canons of Islam 
in all his 
deeds. Acting in accordance with the canons of the reli­
gion of Islam he led the country and people by the way 
of truth of Islam and Shariat. “The Code” reads: “ Every 
state plunged into the abyss of evil, oppression and dis­
cord must become the target for a just padishah expedition, 
who is urged by the intention of re-establishing the truth 
and eradicating evil. The Almighty Himself will help him 
to liberate these countries from the oppression 
of the 
tyrant, and is sure to pass the reins of the state into his 
h a n d s - . . The state where the m ainstay of S h ariat is w e a ­
kened, the sacred things are not worshipped, 
an d the 
people of the 
true Sultan chosen 
by the Most 
High, 
O ur Maker, are oppressed, m ust rouse him, who has in­
tended to be the zealot of faith and Shariat of M ukham - 
m a d —■ m ay Allah bless him! — and induce him to inva­
ding the bounds of such a state in the role of liberator 
and envoy of A llah”. This principle guided not only Ti­
mur, but all supreme Islamic rulers as well.
In short, the aggressive tendencies of conquests were 
the law for the monarchs of that time. But these aims 
were pursued under the mottoes of cleansing people of
6 -2 5 2

immorality, of fighting for the purity of Islam, and libera­
ting people from the local ru le rs’ oppression and ty r a n ­
ny. As soon as the country w a s already conquered, still 
another task w as added to those mentioned — it w as tospread Islam a m o n g the local population and m a in ta in 
the laws of ShariatThe first cam paig n of Timur aimed at subjecting oth­
er countries 
w as conducted 
in 1381; 
it w a s 
w a g e d 
a g ainst Malik Ghiyasiddin Pir Ali II, the ruler of H erat 
(1370— 1381). The point is that H erat and Khorasan were 
of great military, strategic importance — as it wrere, th ey 
were a sort of bridgehead for the offensive upon 
Iran, 
Iraq, Syria an d other countries. Timur 
w as willing 
to 
settle the issue peacefully, and with this aim 
he 
sent 
Em ir Khoji Saifiddin to H erat invinting the ruler to the 
kurultai. However, the ruler of H e ra t did not make his 
appearance at the kurultai, and detained 
the envoy of 
Timur. It w as an unequivocal challenge to a war. In the 
w ay of reply to this, in April 1380, Amir Timur 
desp at­
ched to the left bank of the A m u d arya ten regiments u n ­
der Em irzade P irm u k h a m m u d Jakhanghir. The prince took 
hold of the regions of Balkh, Shiribergan and Badkhiz 
in that campaign. In F ebruary 1381, Timur himelf began 
an offensive on Khorasan; Serax, Jami, Kausia and other 
cities 
surrendered to him without a battle, and H erat 
w as taken after five days of fighting ag a in st the besie­
ged city. At the sam e time the cities of Tus and K elat 
were seized.
In the year 783 of the H ijra (1381), Amir Tim ur sei­
zed Turshiz and Sabzevar, besides Kelat. In 785 of the 
H ijra (1383), he led his troops tow ards Seiston and cap­
tured its fortified citadels of Zireh, Zava, F a ra h and Bust. 
A year later he began a m arch on A strab ad and A zer­
baijan, and took possession of the cities of Amul, Sari, 
Sultaniya and Tebriz. So, from 
1381 to 1384, Timur suc­
ceeded in conquering a better part of Iran.
After this Timur m ade m ajor marches on Iran, Azer­
baijan, Iraq and Syria three times. These m arches gge 
known in history as a three-year, five-year, and sevenyear war.
During the three-year w ar (1386— 1388), Azerbaijan 
and Pharce were conquered. T im u r’s forces then seized 
the lands of Turkmen that were under authority of Kara 
Yusuf, that is, southern A zerbaijan 
and the 
northern.

p art of Iran, Georgia, Armenia and the lands in the vici­
nity of lake Van.
In the period of 1392— 1396, during the five-year war, 
a military expedition w a s organized ag a in st Iran, A zer­
baijan and the Persian part of Iraq. The reason w as dis­
turbance in M a z a n d a ra n , Pharce and Luristan, as well 
as the hostile actions of these regions 
to w ard s Timur. 
The important result of the five-year w a r w as the aboli­
tion of the M usaffarid and Jalayirid dynasties.
In fact, there w as absolutely no need to begin a c a m ­
paign against India. According to the words of Ghiyasiddin Ali, the author of 
“ Diary of T im u r’s C am p aig n in 
In d ia ”, written in 
1399— 1403, the reason for this w ar 
w as the fact that, through the Indian R uler’s 
careless­
ness, the foundations of Islam and S hariat were d a m a ­
ged, and the activities of the infidels (Herbs) and 
ene­
mies of Islam enhanced. The Indian w a r continued ele­
ven months — from M ay 1398 to March 3, 1399. The Tim urid troops returned from India with plenty of 
loot. 
There were 120 fighting elephants a m o n g the trophies. 
A part of these Tim ur left at the disposal of the troops, 
the rest were used in the construction wrork then condu­
cted in Sa m a rk a n d and Shakhrisabz.
The seven-year w a r 
(1399— 1404) w7as the greatest 
w ar conducted by Amir Timur. The outcome of this w ar 
w as the subjection of such m ajor Syrian cities as Khalab

of Ubulistan of the A rabian part 
of Iraq, 
as 
well as 
B a g h d a d and a greater part of Turkey. At that time the

also invaded Georgia.
In the course of the seven-year w a r Timur won vic­
tories not only over Turkey, but also over one of the g re ­
atest Generals of the world at that time — S ultan Bayazid Yildirim I (1389— 1402). The crucial battle between 
the two military leaders took place on July 20, 1402, at 
A nkara, in the district of Chibukabad. “Zafar-nam e” n a r ­
rates about the course of the battle in detail: the odds 
were now in favour of Bavazid Yildirim, now in favour 
of Amir Timur. However, the outcome of the battle w as 
decided by the tactics that Timurbek 
employed 
in the 
h a rd e st m oments of the combat: deceiving 
the enemy, 
Timur dealt a sudden blow where it h a d been least ex­
pected. H av in g lost the battle, Bayazid w a s forced to re­
treat, but he did 
not m a n a g e 
to escape. 
The 
gre a t

general received Bayazid-khan with 
due 
respect 
an d 
honoured him, offered him 
a seat beside himself, put a

embroidered in gold on his shoulders, g a ­
ve him precious

left for the city of Ak-shakhar, 
an d there 
he died 
on 
M arch 9, 1 4 0 3 / L earn in g about it, Timur immediately set 
off to Ak-shakhar to express his condolences. 
Then he 
expressed his sym pathy and respect to the children an d 
kin of the late and aw ard ed them with precious 
gifts. 
A m o n g all the sons of Yildirim he especially m arked M u ­
sa, an d having given him valuable presents, he entrusted 
him with the fate of the Turkish state. 
Then, 
too, on 
Am ir T im ur’s proposal, the body of Bayazid Yildirim w as 
taken from the Shaih M a kh m u d Khairan in Ak-shakhar 
an d transferred to the city of Bursa, where it w a s com ­
m itted to 
earth in a mausoleum, which had been erec­

On coming 
to S a m a r k a n d (1404), Tim ur 
b e g a n to 
put into life his long-nurtured dream — he started pre­
p arations for a big expedition to China. The point w as 
th a t the Em perors of China had still been laying claims 
to the territory of M a v e ra n n a k h r and T urkestan and de­
m a n d e d annual contributions. That w as why Timur had 
long intended 
to solve the m atter and did so in the end. 
He embarked on his expedition on November 27, 1404, 
with a 200 tho u san d -stro n g army gathered from all the 
regions and lands. However, this military expedition to 
China was 
never completed, 
for he died on 
F eb ru ary 
18, 1405.
The w ars w ag ed by Timur, like the w a rs w a g e d by 
other feudal rulers, caused immense d a m a g e to the peop­
le of the conquered countries. Timur and his emirs re ­
tu rn ed from other lands with huge booty. Together with 
the spoils, they brought prom inent scientists an d scho­
lars, skilful artisans, musicians, etc. to S a m a rk a n d , Shakhrisabz and Bukhara. However, Timur 
differed 
from 
other conquerors in that he spent the wealth he gained 
on bettering the cities and settlements of his land. A wellknown Russian historian A. Yu. Yakubovsky compares 
Timur with M a k h m ud 
Gaznovi, 
another 
o u tsta n d in g 
conqueror (998— 1030). The in num erable wealth that w as 
p a ssin g through M a k h m u d ’s h a n d s as w a r trophies had 
never been conducive to the economic life of the Gaznovi 
state, whereas it w as different with Timur. As a good,

thrifty master, he drew all that w as of value to M a v e r ­
annakhr, the centre of his v ast kingdom, which only he 
alone kept in hand. Here he brought not only th in g s of 
m aterial value, but also, still more, he used experts in 
various kinds of crafts, artists, architects, scientists and 
scholars — and he did it not because M a v e ra n n a k h r w a s 
poor in this respect, but because he believed 
that 
the 
greater the inflow of culture and people of culture, the 
more culture and science would flourish. Not only did he 
enlarge and make more convenient and beautiful the ci­
ties and towns of M a v e ra n n a k h r and Turkestan, but he 
had built m any cities of the conquered countries anew. 
For example, he restored the cities of 
B ag h d ad , Derbend, Bailakan; 
roadside fortresses, camps, bridges and 
irrigation systems destroyed or dam aged in the war. To 
m ake a long story short, he w as striving to rem ain in 
people's memory by erecting one or two g ra n d buildings 
in every big city. W hat is of particular importance, he 
put an end to the feudal disunity and anarchy in Iran, 
Azerbaijan and Iraq, restored the ancient roads connec­
ting E ast and West. In its turn, this enabled the coun­
tries to restore their economic, trade and cultural ties 
broken as a result of feudal wars. This is his great con­
tribution to the economic and cultural development not 
of M av e ra n n a k h r alone, but the countries 
of the Far, 
Central and Near East, to the cause of b ringing closer 
the peoples of these countries.
AMIR TIMUR — PATRON OF SCIENCE AND 
CULTURE
Amir Timur m ade a great contribution 
in the resto­
ration of national economy, 
rebuilding cities and c re a t­

In 1371— 72 were restored the walls and the citadel 
of Sam arkand, Shakhriston and its six gates (Shahzada,

By Tim ur’s orders, two big four-storeyed buildings were 
put up in the Ark (citadel) of S a m a rk a n d — Kok-sarai

and Buston-sarai. Kok-sarai w as the seat 
of the S tate 
Treasury, work-shops and the prison, and Buston-sarai 
w a s the m ain residence of Padishah. U n de r Timur the 
city streets were rebuilt and the m arkets 
m a de 
more 
convenient. Claviho writes: “ In S a m a r k a n d every year 
goods are sold which were brought from China, India, 
T a ta r s ta n 
(Dasht-i-kipchak — B. A.) and other places; 
as well as from 
the 
richest 
S a m ark an d . 
As 
there 
were 
no 
special 
rows 
where it w as 
convenient 
to

a street 
across 
the city, to set up shops and tents for selling goods and 
w ares on both sides.”
Amir Timur gave a lot of attention to creating public 
services and utilities in the places sacred for every M o s ­
lem. One of these w as Shah-i Zinda, where 
in T im u r’s 
time were buried Ulja-ayim, Tugluk tekin, his elder sis­

k an d — a covered 
m arket row. The famous R ukhabad 
(the burial-vault of Burkhan-iddin So g arji), the tomb of

Thanks to endeavours of Timur, not a small num ber

dervishes’

and bath-houses. A m o n g these is Bibi-khanum M a d ra s a h

(1399— 1404).
In the vicinity of S a m a r k a n d some newr towns were

with a deep 
sense, and political impications. By this Tim ur m ean t to

Within 1378— 1404 Tim ur created wonderful g ard en s 
in S a m a rk a n d and all around it: Bag-i bishikht, Bag-i 
dilkusha, Bag-i shamal, Bag-i buldi, Bag-i nad, Bag-i 
ja k h a n n u m a , Bag-i takhti karachi and Bag-i davlatabad.

In the middle of these g ardens were erected magnificent, 
luxurious palaces, and in front of them 
were laid out 
flower-gardens. The estates built by Timur and the Tim u rids were beautiful and resplendent. 
As a rule, the 
palaces were two storeys high, with marble columns; the

interior walls — with landscapes and draw ings of im ­
m ense beauty. Ibn A rabshah testifies:” On the walls of 
the palace were 
depicted his divans (State Council ses­
sions), and his portraits in different poses and events... 
in battles; pictures of him at official solemn occasions, 
in conference with tsars, emirs, sayids, ulems and with 
great people; in some of them the sultans stan d before

lecting feasts.”
On the whole, at the time of Timur, S a m a r k a n d w as 
transformed into a rich and beautiful city. Here are the 
words of Khafizi Abru, a historian: 
“ The 
S a m a rk a n d 
formerly m ade of mud-bricks w as re-built by Timur, who 
erected edifices of stone”.
Amir Timur also saw to installing public services and 
amenities in his native city of Shakhrisabz. He recons­
tructed the destroyed town-walls, fortifications for de­
fence, tombs of the saints, built g ra n d palaces, mosques, 
m adrasahs, burial-vaults, Chorsu, bazaars and bath-hou­
ses. Aksarai, built in 1380— 1404, and the family vault
— the building of D ar us-saadat, built in 1380, still stand 
intact to our daysAmir Timur also arra n g e d public facilities and am e­
nities in the cities of Yassi (present-day Turkestan) and 
Bukhara. In 1398, by his injunction, a magnificent buil­
ding was constructed over the grave of the great father 
of all the Turkic people and poet Khoja A khm ad Yassavi — a m ausoleum with two domes. In 1388 Timur re­
constructed the city of Banaket ruined during the inva­
sion of Genghiz-khan, later known under the nam e of 
S hahrukhia (nam ed so by Timur himself in honour of 
his junior son Shahrukh).
t h a n k s to support and efforts of Timur, applied arts

a r t o: the M av e ra n n a k h r m asters of the brush.

B agisham ali

In the epoch of Timur widely developed were scien­
ces, particularly, such branches as theology, law, m a th e ­
matics, astronomy, medicine, history, philosophy, litera­

history of T im u r” ) by Ibn Arabshah, there are laconic,

of

Jalalid d in A khm ad 
al-Khorezmi,

As a result, the representatives of each of the w o n d e r­

ful sciences and rare arts were gathered in Samarkand-. 
Ibn A rabshah recalls.
In conclusion, it m ay be said that, in contradistinc­

E P I L O G U E
A lot can be said and written about T im u r’s 
social 
and political activities and 
life, 
full of 
danger 
and 
strong emotions. W hat is told in this booklet is 
just a 
small drop in the sea.
Specifically, in the booklet we have not touched upon 
a very important question — Timur S ta te ’s trade, econo­
mic activities and foreign policy. The state 
created by 
Timur w as world-known. It w as recognized 
by 
m any 
m ajor countries, beginning with China and ending with 
England. A lot 
of data have already been accumulated, 
but they are not sufficient to expound the issues in de­
tail. For the time being, we are only acquainted with just 
a small part of T im u r’s and his successors’ 
correspon­
dence, deposited in the archives and libraries of Venice, 
Spain, France, Britain and Turkey. It is necessary to get 
access to and study all these letters, collections of Timur 
and the T im urids’ letters, as well as the valuable studies 
of a m ajor French orientologist Silvester de Sassi, de­
voted to the correspondence of Timur and 
M iranshah 
with the French kings, the work of the Italian historian 
M arino Sanudo “ Lives of Venetian D o g es” (Medioloni, 
1733). Of importance are also the works of J. Delaville 
on the foreign policy 
of Bizantium and W estern coun­
tries at the end of the 14th— the beginning of the 15th 
centuries (Paris, 1886), a valuable composition of a reli­
gious figure of the Vatikan, Archbishop Ioann (Johann) 
“ Timur and the Life of his C o u rt” (Paris, 
1894) 
and 
other similar works. Without doing this work, it will be 
difficult to speak about the foreign policy of Timur. Rese­
arch an d expounding of this important and difficult s u b ­
ject is an urgent task facing scholars — historians.

Nachinaya s 1227 goda, Chagatayskiy ulus predstavlyal

v tyajelom

tanmachi** i daruxachi***. No Chagataidы ne podchinilis

razvernul upornuyu borbu za osvobojdenie ulusa iz-pod 
gneta Zolotoy ordы. Po svedeniyam, privedennыm isto­

pod gorodom Otrarom.
Pravda, i posle etogo Chagatayskiy ulus vse-taki ne

BORBA S MONGOLAMI
V 1362 godu Tugluk Timur-xan speshno pokinul M a­
verannaxr, peredav vlast v strane svoemu sыnu Ylyasxodja-oglanu. Prichinoy tomu bыli nespokoynaya obsta­
novka v Mogolistane i myatej gruppы emirov. Ne uspel 
xan peresech reku 
Sыrdaryu, kak Ilyasxodja-oglan, 
vmeste s emirom Bekchikom i drugimi blizkimi emira­
mi, sgovorilis udalit Timurbeka ot gosudarstvennыx 
del, a pri udobnom sluchae i unichtojit yego fizicheski. 
Intrigi vse bolee usilivalis i prinyali opasnыy x a­
rakter. Ochutivshis v takix usloviyax, Timurbek vmeste 
s gruppoy yedinomыshlennikov i slug vыnujden bыl po­
kinut xanskiy dvorets i uyti v gorы...
V te godы, kogda v uluse tsarili razdroblennost i 
neustoychivost, soshlis dva deyatelnыx i predpriim­
chivыx emira: Timurbek 
i 
vnuk pokoynogo Kazagana 
emir Xusayn. V poiskax schastya i udachi oni ushli v sto­
ronu Xorezma. No udachi ne dobilis. Bolee togo, v sra­
jenii pod Xivoy oni poterpeli porajenie ot pravitelya 
tex zemel Tavakkala-kongurota i s ostatkami svoix vo­
inov i slug vыnujdenы bыli otstupit vglub pustыni. 
Dolgoe vremya probiralis oni cherez jestokie peski i, 
nakonets, kogda uje vыshli k aulu Maxmudi v oblasti, 
podvlastnoy Maxanu, bыli vzyatы v plen lyudmi Alibeka 
Djanikurbana. 62 dnya proderjal ix v temnitse Alibek. 
Soglasno svedeniyam istorika Sharafiddina Ali Yazdp, 
Alibek namerevalsya prodat Timurbeka i Xusayna iran­
skim kuptsam. No v te dni cherez Maxan ne proshel ni odin 
karavan. Uzniki chudom bыli spasenы starshim bratom 
Alibeka emirom Muxammad-bekom.
V 1361 — 1364 godax Timurbek i emir Xusayn jili 
na yujnom beregu Amudari v oblastyax Kaxmard, Daragez, Arsif i Balx i veli protiv mongolov partizanskuyu 
voynu. Uluchiv moment, oni perexodili na pravыy- bereg 
reki, napadali na mongolskie voyska, stoyavshie v Ter­
meze, u Jeleznыx vorot, v Xuzare i Keshe. V 1363-— 1364 
godax oni daje oderjali ryad pobed nad mongolami. A 
osenyu 1362 goda Timurbek s emirom Xusaynom uchastvo­
vali v voyne protiv silnыx i zleyshix vragov Malika 
Kutbiddina na storone etogo seistanskogo* pravitelya. 
Timurbek, privыkshiy vsegda bыt v pervыx ryadax sra­
* 
Seistan — oblast, raspolojennaya mejdu Afganistanom i 
Iranom.

jayushixsya, v odnom nz boev bыl ranen v pravuyu ruku n

mejdu soboy i posadili na prestol ulusa Kabul-shaxa

Mongolы, odnako, ne smirilis s etim porajeniem.

Emir Xusain,

OBRAZOVANIE GOSUDARSTVA TIMURA
V mesyatse tirmax 771 goda xidjrы (v iyule 1370 goda) 
Amir Timur sozval v Samarkande bolshoy kurultay. Na 
nego, v sootvetstvii s sushestvuyushimi obыchayami, bыli

kak obrazovaniya tsentralnoy vlasti, tak i sozdaniya ar­

Do 1377 goda ulus Djuchi takje bыl razdelen na dva

VOENNЫE POXODЫ V D R U GI Ye STRANЫ, IX 
PRIChINЫ I POSLEDSTVIYa
V period s 1381 po 1404 godы Amir Timur neskolko 
raz sovershal poxodы na Iran, Azerbaydjan, Irak, Si­
riyu i Indiyu, a takje v kraya gruzin i drugie zemli Kav­
kaza. Yesli podoyti k otsenke yego s pozitsiy zakonov ob­
shestvenno-ekonomicheskogo razvitiya 
i 
ideologii togo 
vremeni, to mojno uvidet, chto dlya etogo sushestvovali 
ob'ektivnыe prichinы.
Vo-pervыx, vse padishaxi, xanы i imperatorы yavlya­
lis vыxodtsami iz gospodstvuyushego klassa. A raz tak, 
to oni sledovali ukazaniyam etogo klassa, obyazanы bыli 
slujit yego tselyam i interesam. V protivnom sluchae ix 
ne stali bы derjat u vlasti, svergli bы s prestola. I 
etot gospodstvuyushiy klass odobryal tex padishaxov, xa­
nov i imperatorov, kotorыe postoyanno veli bolshie 
voynы, pokoryali vse novыe p novыe stranы, ibo tolko 
takim putem oni mogli rasshiryat svoi vladeniya, ob­
retat bogatuyu dobыchu.
Vo-ztorыx, 
v te vremena rasshiryat svoi vladeniya 
schitalos u praviteley prestijnыm, bogatыrskim ob­
razom jizni, t.e. rыtsarstvom. Yesli mыslenno okinut 
vzorom istoriyu, to uvidim, chto ne tolko Timur, no i 
mnogie velikie vlastiteli, takie kak Dariy, Aleksandr 
Makedonskiy, Yuliy Tsezar, 
Chingnzxan, priderjiva­
lis etogo puti. Yesli posmotret poblije, to i Napo­
leon Bonapart, Petr I i posleduyushie russkie tsari 
shli etim je putem. Ne zrya bыlo skazano, chto «Tsariten Boga na zemle!» Odnako takie zavoevateli, kak 
Dariy I, Aleksandr Makedonskiy, Chingizxan i Amir 
Timur schitali, chto «raz na svete odin Bog, to i yavlya­
yushiysya yego tenyu na obitaemoy chasti zemli doljen 
bыt odin tsar». «Ya skazal sebe, — govoril Amir Timur, — chto Bog odin, i u nego net tovarisha! A raz tak, 
to i vlastelin doljen bыt odin. Odin padishax!» 
I kak raz v eto vremya ko mne voshel Baba Ali-shax i 
skazal: «Ey, Timur! Vsevыshniy ukazal, chtv yesli na 
zemle i nebe budet dva boga, razrushitsya mir!». Ya posle­
doval yego slovam. Stal gadat na svyashennom Korane i

Koroche govorya, zavoevatelskie tendentsii stali za­

AMIR T I M U R — POKROVITEL NAUKI I 
KULTURЫ
Amir Timur 
vnes bolshoy vklad v vosstanovlenie 
narodnogo xozyaystva, v perestroyku gorodov i blagoust­
roystvo stranы, prishedshix v upadok v rezultate mno­
goletney feodalnoy razdroblennosti i mejdousobnыx 
voyn. Izvestno, chto ochen mnogie goroda Maverannaxra, 
razrushennыe posle nashestviya Chingizxana, lejali v raz­
valinax ili bыli v polurazrushennom sostoyanii.
V 1371—72 godax 
bыli vosstanovlenы razrushennaya 
-krepost Samarkanda i yego stenы, Shaxristan i yego shest 
vorot (Shaxzade, 
Axanin, Feruza, Suzangaran, Karizgax. Chorsu). Po prikazu Timura v arke Samarkanda bыli 
postroenы dva bolshix chetыrexetajnыx zdaniya: Koksaray i Buston-saray. V Kok-sarae bыli raspolojenы 
gosudarstvennaya kazna, 
masterskie, tyurma, a Bustonsaray bыl glavnoy rezidentsiey padishaxa. Pri Timure 
zanovo bыli postroenы gorodskie ulitsы, blagoustroenы 
bazarы. Kak pishet Klavixo: «V Samarkande yejegodno 
prodayutsya tovarы, privezennыe iz Kitaya, Indii, Tatar­
stana (Dasht-i kipchaka — B. A.) i drugix mest, a takje 
iz samogo bogatogo 
tsarstva 
Samarkanda.

torgovat, Timurbek prikazal prolojit cherez 
gorod

Osoboe vnimanie Amir Timur udelil blagoustroy­
stvu svyashennыx dlya kajdogo musulmanina mest. Odnim 
iz takix mest bыl Shaxi-Zinda, mavzoley, gde vo vreme­
na Timura bыli poxoronenыUldja-ayim, Tugluk tekin,

sestra Shirin-beka, nad mogilami kotorыx bыli vozve­
denы grobnitsы. Izvestno i to, chto po ukazaniyu starshey 
jenы Amira Timura Tuman-aga v Shaxi-Zinda bыl po­
stroen ryad zdaniy: mechet, obitel dervishey, usыpal­

Podvedya itog, mojno skazat, chto Amir Timur, v ot­

kulture i iskusstvu, i pokrovitelstvoval im.

POSLESLOVIE
O polnoy opasnostey i dushevnыx volneniy jizni i 
obshestvenno-politicheskoy deyatelnosti Timura mojno 
govorit i pisat ochen mnogo. To, chto skazano 
v etoy 
broshyure — eto vsego lish kaplya v more.
V broshyure, v chastnosti, mы ne ostanavlivalis na 
odnom vajnom 
voprose — na torgovo-ekonomicheskoy 
i 
vneshnepoliticheskoy deyatelnosti gosudarstva Timura. 
Kak izvestno, sozdannoe Timurom gosudarstvo znali vo 
vsem mire. Yego priznali mnogie krupnыe stranы, nachi­
naya ot Kitaya i konchaya Angliey. Ob etom sobrano nemalo 
svedeniy, no ix nedostatochno, chtobы podrobno izlojit 
etot vopros. Mы poka znakomы lish s ochen maloy chastyu 
perepiski Timura i yego preemnikov, xranyasheysya v ar­
xivax i bibliotekax Venetsii, Ispanii, Frantsii, Ang­
lii i Turtsii. Neobxodimo poluchit i izuchit vse eti 
pisma, sborniki pisem Timura i timuridov, a takje 
tsennыe 
issledovaniya krupnogo 
frantsuzskogo uchenogovostokoveda Silvestra de Sasi, posvyashennыe perepis­
ke Timura i Miranshaxa 
s frantsuzskimi korolyami, i 
proizvedenie italyanskogo 
istorika Marino Sanudo 
«Jizn 
venetsianskix 
dojey» (Medioloni, 1733 g.). 
Ochen vajnы trudы J. Delavilla o vneshney politike 
Vizantii i zapadnыx stran v kontse XIV i nachale XV 
vekov (Parij, 1886 g.), a takje sostoyashee iz 24-x glav 
tsennoe sochinenie 
religioznogo deyatelya iz Vatikana 
arxiepiskopa Ioanna «Timur i jizn yego dvora» (Pa­
rij, 1894 g.) i t. d. Ne provedya etoy rabotы, trudno 
budet vesti rech o vneshney politike Timura. Issle­
dovanie i raskrыtie etoy vajnoy i trudnoy temы yav­
lyaetsya neotlojnoy zadachey, stoyashey pered uchenыmiistorikami.
